My primary doctor noticed this cycle and suggested I talk to someone before it gets any worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'eating way too much' and 'making up for it' - do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: By \"eating way too much,\" I mean these late-night episodes where I eat massive amounts of food on autopilot until I'm uncomfortably full. To \"make up for it\" the next day, I force myself onto long, punishing walks and restrict my food to just coffee or an apple. I have never made myself vomit or used laxatives; for me, it is strictly about the excessive exercise and fasting to try and balance things out.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating way too much happen, and do you feel a lack of control during these episodes?\n\nPatient: These late-night eating episodes happen at least once or twice a week. When they start, it feels like a switch clicks off in my brain and I go on autopilot, where my hands just keep reaching for food even though I'm telling myself to stop. I only really snap out of it when I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or guilt after these episodes of eating way too much, or is it more about the physical discomfort and your attempt to compensate through exercise and fasting?"
  }
]

While there is physical discomfort, the mental distress and guilt are much more overwhelming. I look in the mirror and think I'm getting "soft and useless," feeling like I don't deserve a real meal until I "balance the books." It completely ruins my mood and leads me to cancel plans with friends just so I can focus on "correcting" what I ate.

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Hello. I keep swinging between eating way too much and then trying to ‘make up for it’ with these harsh rules and routines, and it’s starting to take over my days. My primary doctor noticed this cycle and suggested I talk to someone before it gets any worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'eating way too much' and 'making up for it' - do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: By \"eating way too much,\" I mean these late-night episodes where I eat massive amounts of food on autopilot until I'm uncomfortably full. To \"make up for it\" the next day, I force myself onto long, punishing walks and restrict my food to just coffee or an apple. I have never made myself vomit or used laxatives; for me, it is strictly about the excessive exercise and fasting to try and balance things out.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating way too much happen, and do you feel a lack of control during these episodes?\n\nPatient: These late-night eating episodes happen at least once or twice a week. When they start, it feels like a switch clicks off in my brain and I go on autopilot, where my hands just keep reaching for food even though I'm telling myself to stop. I only really snap out of it when I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or guilt after these episodes of eating way too much, or is it more about the physical discomfort and your attempt to compensate through exercise and fasting?\n\nPatient: While there is physical discomfort, the mental distress and guilt are much more overwhelming. I look in the mirror and think I'm getting \"soft and useless,\" feeling like I don't deserve a real meal until I \"balance the books.\" It completely ruins my mood and leads me to cancel plans with friends just so I can focus on \"correcting\" what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you engage in these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, in relation to the binge eating episodes, and do you feel these compensatory behaviors are effective in reducing your guilt and distress?\n\nPatient: I go on those long walks and restrict my food at least once or twice a week, always the day immediately following a late-night episode. They don't actually reduce my distress at all; instead, they feel like a harsh punishment that makes me feel more out of control and completely takes over my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fluctuations in your weight over time due to these cycles of binge eating and compensatory behaviors?"

When they start, it feels like a switch clicks off in my brain and I go on autopilot, where my hands just keep reaching for food even though I'm telling myself to stop. I only really snap out of it when I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or guilt after these episodes of eating way too much, or is it more about the physical discomfort and your attempt to compensate through exercise and fasting?\n\nPatient: While there is physical discomfort, the mental distress and guilt are much more overwhelming. I look in the mirror and think I'm getting \"soft and useless,\" feeling like I don't deserve a real meal until I \"balance the books.\" It completely ruins my mood and leads me to cancel plans with friends just so I can focus on \"correcting\" what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you engage in these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, in relation to the binge eating episodes, and do you feel these compensatory behaviors are effective in reducing your guilt and distress?\n\nPatient: I go on those long walks and restrict my food at least once or twice a week, always the day immediately following a late-night episode. They don't actually reduce my distress at all; instead, they feel like a harsh punishment that makes me feel more out of control and completely takes over my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fluctuations in your weight over time due to these cycles of binge eating and compensatory behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, my primary doctor was actually the one who first pointed out the weight fluctuations during a recent checkup, which is what prompted him to suggest I see a specialist. Personally, I've noticed my clothes fitting differently and I feel like I'm getting soft, which just makes me feel incredibly frustrated and out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the frequency or severity of your binge eating episodes is more closely tied to your emotional state, such as stress or anxiety, or is it more related to external factors like social situations or certain types of food?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really seem to be about social situations or specific types of food. Since I cut back my work hours, I’ve lost my daily structure and often get so absorbed in my painting that I skip meals during the day. That leads to me overeating late at night when the house is quiet and I finally realize how hungry I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your binge eating episodes are always followed by these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, or are there instances where you binge eat without attempting to compensate afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are almost always followed by those behaviors. The guilt and the urge to 'balance the books' are so strong that every single time I have one of those late-night kitchen raids, I feel compelled to force myself into a long, punishing walk and barely eat the next day."
  }

I keep swinging between eating way too much and then trying to ‘make up for it’ with these harsh rules and routines, and it’s starting to take over my days. My primary doctor noticed this cycle and suggested I talk to someone before it gets any worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'eating way too much' and 'making up for it' - do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: By \"eating way too much,\" I mean these late-night episodes where I eat massive amounts of food on autopilot until I'm uncomfortably full. I have never made myself vomit or used laxatives; for me, it is strictly about the excessive exercise and fasting to try and balance things out.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating way too much happen, and do you feel a lack of control during these episodes?\n\nPatient: These late-night eating episodes happen at least once or twice a week. When they start, it feels like a switch clicks off in my brain and I go on autopilot, where my hands just keep reaching for food even though I'm telling myself to stop. I only really snap out of it when I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or guilt after these episodes of eating way too much, or is it more about the physical discomfort and your attempt to compensate through exercise and fasting?\n\nPatient: While there is physical discomfort, the mental distress and guilt are much more overwhelming. I look in the mirror and think I'm getting \"soft and useless,\" feeling like I don't deserve a real meal until I \"balance the books.\" It completely ruins my mood and leads me to cancel plans with friends just so I can focus on \"correcting\" what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you engage in these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, in relation to the binge eating episodes, and do you feel these compensatory behaviors are effective in reducing your guilt and distress?\n\nPatient: I go on those long walks and restrict my food at least once or twice a week, always the day immediately following a late-night episode. They don't actually reduce my distress at all; instead, they feel like a harsh punishment that makes me feel more out of control and completely takes over my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fluctuations in your weight over time due to these cycles of binge eating and compensatory behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, my primary doctor was actually the one who first pointed out the weight fluctuations during a recent checkup, which is what prompted him to suggest I see a specialist. Personally, I've noticed my clothes fitting differently and I feel like I'm getting soft, which just makes me feel incredibly frustrated and out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the frequency or severity of your binge eating episodes is more closely tied to your emotional state, such as stress or anxiety, or is it more related to external factors like social situations or certain types of food?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really seem to be about social situations or specific types of food. Since I cut back my work hours, I’ve lost my daily structure and often get so absorbed in my painting that I skip meals during the day. That leads to me overeating late at night when the house is quiet and I finally realize how hungry I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your binge eating episodes are always followed by these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, or are there instances where you binge eat without attempting to compensate afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are almost always followed by those behaviors. The guilt and the urge to 'balance the books' are so strong that every single time I have one of those late-night kitchen raids, I feel compelled to force myself into a long, punishing walk and barely eat the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're able to stop or reduce these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, without feeling like you're giving in to your binge eating, or do you feel like they're absolutely necessary to make up for the binge eating episodes?\n\nPatient: They feel absolutely necessary, and it feels impossible to just stop or ease up on them. I feel this overwhelming need to 'correct' what I ate and 'balance the books,' and I don't feel like I deserve a real meal or a break until I've done so. If I don't stick to these harsh rules, it feels like I'm letting things get completely out of control."

Personally, I've noticed my clothes fitting differently and I feel like I'm getting soft, which just makes me feel incredibly frustrated and out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the frequency or severity of your binge eating episodes is more closely tied to your emotional state, such as stress or anxiety, or is it more related to external factors like social situations or certain types of food?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t really seem to be about social situations or specific types of food. Since I cut back my work hours, I’ve lost my daily structure and often get so absorbed in my painting that I skip meals during the day. That leads to me overeating late at night when the house is quiet and I finally realize how hungry I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your binge eating episodes are always followed by these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, or are there instances where you binge eat without attempting to compensate afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are almost always followed by those behaviors. The guilt and the urge to 'balance the books' are so strong that every single time I have one of those late-night kitchen raids, I feel compelled to force myself into a long, punishing walk and barely eat the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're able to stop or reduce these compensatory behaviors, such as the long walks and restrictive eating, without feeling like you're giving in to your binge eating, or do you feel like they're absolutely necessary to make up for the binge eating episodes?\n\nPatient: They feel absolutely necessary, and it feels impossible to just stop or ease up on them. I feel this overwhelming need to 'correct' what I ate and 'balance the books,' and I don't feel like I deserve a real meal or a break until I've done so. If I don't stick to these harsh rules, it feels like I'm letting things get completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned not using laxatives or vomiting, but have you ever used diuretics or any other methods to try and purge or compensate for the binge eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I have never used diuretics, water pills, or any other purging methods to make up for the eating. My only way of trying to \"balance the books\" is through those long, punishing walks and strictly restricting my food the next day to just coffee and an apple."
  }
]

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
